FOR YOU TO READ 

You've come far since you learned to spell. You've made habits that you'll be able to depend on for the rest of your life. You really need only one more piece of spelling advice, but that's a very important one. 

Here it is. Everyone makes a spelling mistake now and then, but a good student tries not to make the same one twice. When you need to correct a mistake you have made, or keep yourself from making one, use the set of instructions below. acceptable 

STEP ONE 

See how the word really looks.

Look well at it to see it as a whole and to see what letters are in it. 

Then, if you don't know what it means, find it in your dictionary before you go any further. iii

ak.sep tabl 

STEP TWO 

Say the word and hear how it sounds. 

Pronounce it carefully without looking at it. If it has more than one syllable, listen for them. 

Then check yourself by looking at the word again. 

If the pronunciation is difficult for you, get further help on pages 58 to 61 and 113 to 116. acceptable רר (נד 

STEP THREE 

Look away and recall how the word looks.

Try to recall clearly how it looks and what letters are in it. Saying the letters softly may help. 

Then check with the word itself. iv

acceptable acceptable 

STEP FOUR 

Copy the word correctly on paper. 

Use as much care as you need to write it correctly. 

STEP FIVE 

Write the word without looking at it.

Try to remember the letters and their correct order. 

Then check your spelling with the word itself. 

Use these five steps whenever you need special help in spelling.

TO HELP YOU USE THIS BOOK 

This book will give you several kinds of help in using words effectively. It will help you to become an independent speller and therefore a more effective writer. 

KEEPING MY SPELLING NOTEBOOK 

You will want to keep a Spelling Notebook. You may need to study some words more than once, to spell them right. Your teacher will help you make your Spelling

Notebook for Words I Must Study Again. In it you will spell right any Study List words you spelled wrong in your B and E tests each week, and any other words you spelled wrong in your other writing. Then you will be able to study these words and learn to spell them right. 

Your teacher will show you how to keep a part of your

Spelling Notebook for Words That Are Mine Now. This is the place where you will write all the words you misspelled in your review tests or in your other writing, when you have learned to spell them right.

FIVE THINGS TO DO EACH WEEK 

Here are the five parts of what you do each week.

A. Writing Words 1. Pronounce the Study List words. Your teacher, your dictionary, and pages 58 to 61 and 113 to 116 will help you. 

2. Read the story to find out how the words may be used. If there is a picture, find out what it tells about the story. 

3. In the story, find each Study List word or any word closely related to it. 

4. Write the Study List words on your paper. Pages iii to v will give you help if you need it. Check your work with the Study List. vi

B. Finding Words I Know 

1. Write the Study List words as your teacher pronounces them. Use this opportunity to improve your handwriting. 

2. Check your work with the Study List. Put a check √ after each word in your own list that you spelled right. 

Draw a line through each word you spelled wrong. 

3. If you spelled a word wrong, spell it right in your

Spelling Notebook. Write it under the heading B Test in Words I Must Study Again. Then check again with the Study List. 

C. Looking Into Words 1. Find things to remember about your Study List words by analyzing their spellings. 

2. Read the first thing you are to do when you are 

Looking into Words. Do it carefully. 

3. Analyze your other Study List words as directed. 

4. Use the rest of your time to study the words you missed in Finding Words I Know. Use the help you will find on pages iii to v. 

D. Using Words 

1. Learn more about what your Study List words mean by using them. 

2. Read the first thing you are to do in Using Words. 

Do it carefully. 

3. Then use your other Study List words as directed. 

4. Use the rest of your time to study the words written in your Spelling Notebook under Words I Must Study 

Again.

E. Making Sure I Know 1. Write the Study List words and the Review words as your teacher pronounces them. 

2. Check your words with the right Study Lists. Put a check after each word that you spelled right. Draw a line through each word you spelled wrong. vii

3. Spell your wrong words right in your Spelling Notebook. Write them under the headings New Words and

Review Words in Words I Must Study Again for next week. Then check again with the Study Lists. 

4. You may spell some words wrong in your other writing. Be sure to spell such words right in your Spelling

Notebook under Words from Other Writing in Words I 

Must Study Again. Make sure that you know them.

DEVELOPING INDEPENDENCE 

A good student becomes an independent worker as soon as possible. He loses no time in finding ways and places that will give him help. You can find several such ways and places in this book.

One way to become independent is to make everyday use of Words from Other Writing in correcting your spelling mistakes. Use Words That Are Mine Now to prove to yourself that you are becoming an independent speller. 

Places in this book where you can find extra help are: the five steps for correcting mistakes, pages iii to v; 

Making Our Work Easier, pages 119 and 120; and Dictionary Data, throughout the book. 

WHAT THE STARS MEAN 

Some of the things to do when you are Looking into 

Words and when you are Using Words are marked with stars (*). All students should do those things. Many students should do the other things, too. Your teacher will help you decide which ones you need to do. 

WATCHING HOW I GROW 

You will want to watch yourself growing in spelling. 

Your teacher will show you how to make My Progress 

Record. Mark on it the number of words you spell right in the E test of the new words of each week. 

The teacher will find directions for the use of the Glossary and both general and detailed pedagogic aid in the Teacher's 

Manual for Directed Spelling Activities and Guide to Spelling 

Progress.

GUIDE TO SPELLING PROGRESS GRADE SEVEN 

Take a look back at the words you first learned to spell. Remember how short they were, and how simple? Remember, too, that they seemed hard then, because they were new to you?

Only a few words you meet seem hard to you now. The reason for this is that you've been learning a system for using old words to help you with new ones. Spelling is becoming for you more and more a process of using knowledge that you already have. 

As you continue with spelling, you will be meeting fewer and fewer unfamiliar words. You will find in most new words familiar parts that you have often used before. What you learn about spelling will be interesting facts about languages from which 

English words were borrowed, the way changing letters in a word may change the meaning, and other things that will add to your knowledge of words and their uses. 

From now on you will be turning the old into the new. 1

1. SCHOOLS OF TODAY A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. You will find all the Study List words, with their pronunciation, given in alphabetical order on pages 58 to 61. Then find the Study List words in the story. 

Sometimes the story will use a form of the Study List word, the root of it, or a word made from it. For instance, the word preparation in the story is one of three Study List words made from the word prepare. practical preparation practically prepared planning preparing experience carpenter capable ability render enjoyed industrial student happened services served formerly household numerous

We may be glad we happen to be twentieth-century students. Schools in this country were formerly less practical in numerous ways than those of today. Our modern public school gives vocational preparation in many fields. It renders valuable service by planning studies that will be useful to us in earning a living. 

The boy who wants to be a carpenter gets good experience in the industrial arts shop. Girls with housekeeping ability enjoy household arts courses. Our schools help us to become capable citizens. 

2. Read pages iii to v carefully. Write the new words in alphabetical order on your paper. Check with the Study 

List to be sure you spelled them right.

2. EQUAL OPPORTUNITIES IN EDUCATION A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. Then find them in the story. frequently frequency forms astonish frequent opportunity grammar reasonable religious naturally religion largely equally professor policy community usually considered considering consideration Early American schools were frequently religious in nature. The students were largely boys, since it was not considered important for girls to be educated. 

The course of study usually included grammar, mathematics, and forms of literature, in addition to Bible study. It was thought reasonable that only those boys with the best intelligence should be educated. The old professors would be astonished at our modern schools, with their policy of equal opportunities for all children in every community.

*Dictionary Data 

These dictionary data are facts and information about some of your Study List words. Use them for discussion and study. They will help you learn how to make the best use of your dictionary. con sid'er a/tion (kon sid'er a'shun), n. 

1. Careful thinking or attention. I gave special consideration to the choice of books. 2. Courtesy or kindness. The injured boy should have every consideration. 3. Reason. These are the considerations we must take into account. 4. Payment or reward. We may use the auditorium for a small consideration. gram/mar (grămꞌẽr), n. The science of language structure; the rules governing speaking or writing. Good students find grammar useful in most of their reading and writing.

3. OUR CHANGING COUNTRY A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. Then find them in the story. occur society necessity struggle established grown establish responsible colonies extended represented colony extent represent perfectly extend prosperous otherwise replace settled 海​茶 ​Education represents only one of many changes that have occurred since the thirteen colonies were first established in the New World. The growth of population was responsible for extensive changes in our government. Laws that were perfectly suited to the needs of colonial society came to be far otherwise as larger communities were settled. And as prosperity began to replace the early struggle for existence, changed laws became a necessity.

4. WESTWARD, HO! 

A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. Then find them in the story. solve expansion ambition territory expand sections boundary vast procession youth equipment drift furnished unite develop furnishing prairie agreeable submit agreement The increasing population of our new country needed territory to expand in. Boundary after boundary was pushed back, and vast sections of the West were settled. Men with ambition, courage, and youth furnished themselves with the needed equipment and started across the prairies. As they began to develop their communities, they saw that their only defense against failure was a set of laws to which most of them would agree to submit. Without a good process of lawmaking they would drift into disagreement. 

Then they might fail to unite for the solving of their common problems.

5. HOW WE GOVERN OURSELVES A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. vote✓ connected actual duties affect approve affair connection arise ▾ examine examined highly ​wholly we process organize dealer thereby region organized purposes The duties connected with governing a vast body of free people are highly complex. Problems arise in one region that affect no other part, and the people must deal with these locally. Other questions affect the whole population, and proposed rules need to be examined and approved by most of the people before they are made actual laws. 

To meet these two purposes of government, our country has organized three kinds of governing bodies: national, state, and local. The process of governing our country is thereby made easier, and each citizen, with his vote, may share in its affairs.

6. PAYING OUR WAY A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. funds providing project provide discuss instance discussion generous articles worthy improved improvement supplied 

TIIUN. TON V-LINA 12 m assembly survey taxes benefit discovered assume series We are beginning a survey of projects that benefit entire communities. We shall assemble a series of written articles discussing community needs and how they are met. Our committee has already discovered that funds for community development are provided in two ways. The public contributes through taxes, and private enterprise supplies funds for some types of undertaking. For instance, such public improvements as street paving are paid for through taxation. 

Private individuals sometimes make generous gifts for such worthy causes as community houses or parks. And private companies assume the task of supplying such necessities as electricity to a community.

7. PUBLIC PROPERTY A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. supposed royal liberal official entering expenses expensive modest quality

Brigha entitled membership unpleasant rear chosen thorough ballot lowest operate operating operation

A modern town is a pleasant place. It gives its citizens liberal services of a kind and quality that the old kings in their royal castles never had. Parents rear their children in comfortable homes thoroughly protected by police and fire departments and by city or town officials chosen by public ballot. Families with either modest or large incomes are entitled to equal privileges. The schools are open to all who enter; the streets belong equally to the highest and the lowest. And who do you suppose owns all these good things? Who pays the expenses that support them? Why, you do. We all do. Public property belongs to the citizens, who operate it for all. Our membership fee is our taxes.

8. A GIFT TO THE COMMUNITY A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. stranger visitors presented guests native throughout presents¹ attractive attraction attract spite audience retain ideas there's surprised excitement excited exciting regularly 'As in "giving Christmas presents."

This week there's a lot of excitement throughout the whole school. Last Friday we had visitors at assembly. After the regular exercises our principal presented the guests to the audience. We were considerably surprised at what the two men had to say. 

Though they were strangers to us, they once went to school here. In spite of living somewhere else now, they retain an interest in their native town. They want to make a present to their old school-an attractive park and playground around the school buildings. Their most important idea is that the students themselves shall plan and supervise the work. We're getting busy at once!

9. EFFICIENT PLANNING A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. completed completely steer portion hired appointed sketch propose appoint appointment compose proposed reserve junior informed investigate faculty notify meantime expert Plans for the school park are under way. Several committees have been appointed from the junior and senior high schools. One will investigate costs of labor and materials. Another will make sketches of the proposed layout, showing the portions reserved for playground and for park. A communications committee will keep the school informed of events and notify Mr. Ware and Mr. Davidson as we proceed. A general steering committee is composed of faculty members and the class presidents. 

As soon as plans are completed, a landscaping expert will be hired and work will begin. In the meantime, we are all enjoying school life.

10. DIVIDING THE WORK A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. scrub tackle shovel muscle health shelter demand arrived uncertain hurried arriving factor split threat defense ditch younger ordinary scold partner

When the first settlers arrived in this country, the uncertain living conditions demanded the best efforts of all. Even the weather was a threat to health and comfort. Time was an important factor, and the construction of shelter was hurried. The younger men, with strong muscles, were ready to wield the shovel and ax, to dig the ditches and split the logs. 

The elder partners planned for the general welfare and for the defense against danger. The women, of course, tackled the ordinary housework: the sewing, the scrubbing, the cooking, and maybe some scolding, too! Thus was the work divided.

11. HOW WORK GETS DONE A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. profitable ample possession popular quantity wages difficulty breaking humble advance production factories advanced products employ pleasing paying employer payable employee 

If you need something, you must either make or buy it. The needs of our early American population were few and humble. Each household produced almost all it required, and what was too difficult to make was usually not possessed. 

But with the advance of population came a breaking down of this independence. It was found that factories making just one type of thing could produce that article in quantity at a lower price. So it came about that a man who had money might employ those who had skill. By paying wages to these workers he brought about two pleasing and profitable results. His own money made more money and produced an ample supply of what people wanted.

12. MAN'S WORK A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. destruction happiness happily manual fate namely promote accounts promotion accounting agriculture enemy succeeded contrary mental outstanding durable poultry costume evil

Man has long been writing the account of his own history. It has proved that his work is done to promote two outstanding needs: namely, his comfort and his happiness. To be comfortable he has needed food, clothing, shelter, and protection. So he has learned agriculture and the raising of food animals and poultry. He has learned to fashion durable and practical costumes. He has succeeded in building safe homes. 

He has struggled to conquer the contrary or evil fate that could destroy him through disease or his enemies. 

Most of this work has been manual labor. To be happy, on the other hand, man has used his mind in literature, art, music, and science. And so all his work, manual and mental, serves him well.

13. WORDS IN DISGUISE A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. tale companion clever soul pretend mask puzzle steal decrease¹ hymn records¹ tease protest¹ observation observe fellows fare herd delight delighted ¹Used both as a noun, accented on the first syllable, and as a verb, accented on the second syllable.

Some words seem to delight in dressing up. 

They're jolly fellows and often pretend to tease us with their sounds or spellings. It's sometimes a puzzle to unmask them, unless we're clever enough to watch the companion words that help with the meaning. How quick at observation can you be with the masked words following? 

Sing a hymn to him. Bless my soul, where's my left sole? I heard the herd a mile away. Be fair and pay the fare. Get a steel file for me, but don't steal it.

This is the tale of a tailless bunny. If I protest, will you make a protest, too? Decrease six stitches in each row, for a total decrease of seventy-two stitches. 

The records show who records the minutes of each meeting.

14. HOW WORDS SERVE MAN A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. refer lists sole referred listed relative expression based extreme limited basis extremely limit using disagree accepted acceptable vain disagreeable properly All our language is based upon a relatively limited list of extremely useful words. For the better expression of our ideas, we need various forms of the same word. We make these forms by adding prefixes and suffixes to our basic or root words. Thus, from agree we may make disagree, agreement, and many other words. We accept several meanings for the same word, too. The word vain may mean "useless" or it may mean "conceited." When you see sole you may wonder whether it refers to a single thing or the bottom of a shoe. Words properly used are among man's best servants.

15. SIGNBOARDS IN LANGUAGE A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. arrest assured affectionately despise assure dislike disgust depart export adventures inquire investment import excess impression deduct exceed approach disgrace admire 

When you go shopping, the signs often tell you what the stores sell. Language is much like shopping; you can find word meanings by looking for the signs. 

You know some of them-the prefixes and suffixes. 

Let's see what some prefixes tell.

The prefix ad may mean to, toward. It is sometimes spelled ac, ar, af, ap, as, according to the letter that follows. The prefix in, sometimes spelled im, often means in or into. The prefixes dis, de, and ex, on the contrary, often mean out of, away from, down. 

Find these prefixes in: arrest, affection, adventures, approach, admire, assure; inquire, import, investment, impression; depart, deduct, despise, disgrace, disgust, dislike; export, exceed. Do you see how the prefix signboards help with the meaning?

16. MORE SIGNBOARDS A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. delicious tame burglar nervous nearest scholar ridiculous noble robber celebration steep ambulance relations editor insurance indicate radiator resemble prettiest avoid 

Suffixes are language signboards as useful as prefixes. They, too, help to tell what words mean. By changing the suffix or prefix you can change the meaning of the word. First, of course, you must know suffix and prefix meanings themselves. 

The suffix ous means full of; est means most; tion, ance, or, ar, and er all mean the person or thing doing something. See whether you can locate the suffixes that control the meaning of: delicious, nervous, ridiculous; celebration, indication, relation; prettiest, tamest, nearest, noblest, steepest; editor, radiator, burglar, scholar, robber; ambulance, insurance, resemblance, avoidance.

17. THE MOST REMARKABLE BOOK A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. contains containing contained contents collecting collected compelled compel correctly corrected conclusion constantly constant charm charming counter concrete complain complaint chase

Man has been writing books for centuries. Of all the books he has produced, the dictionary is probably the most remarkable, since with its help other books may be correctly understood. Here are collected words and facts about words that help to give concrete meaning to our daily language. Of course, a dictionary contains much more about every word than we need at any one time; but that is one of its charms. On the chase for one word we constantly catch others. Sometimes they are so interesting that they compel us to stop and follow the new trail to its conclusion. A complaint about the dictionary's large size may be countered by the claim that its benefit is much greater than its bulk.

17. HELPS I CAN FIND IN MY DICTIONARY 

By this time you can use the dictionary in many ways. 

You have discovered that it can be a good servant to you. 

1. Alphabetical Order and Guide Words 

All dictionary entries are put in alphabetical order. The two guide words at the top of each page are a valuable short cut in locating any entry, because they are the first and last words explained on the page. 

2. Spelling and Pronunciation 

The dictionary spells a word for writing and respells it for speaking. Diacritical marks are used in the respelling, to indicate the sounds that all vowels and some consonants have when the word is spoken correctly. Key words at the foot of the dictionary page help in using diacritical marks. 

3. Syllables and Accent Marks 

In writing, a word may have to be divided at the end of a line on which there is not enough room for the whole word. In speaking, pronunciation depends partly on the accented and unaccented syllables. The dictionary divides both the spelling and the respelling of a word into the correct syllables for writing and for speaking. The proper accent is clearly marked in both the spelling and the respelling. 

4. Derived Word Forms 

As students grow older, and do more reading and writing, they begin to learn new words without being taught. Many such words are new forms derived from familiar words. They are made by using prefixes or suffixes, or by joining two words to make one. The spelling and the pronunciation of such new forms can be checked for correctness in the dictionary, which is the final authority. 

5. Meanings 

Language can be neither used nor understood efficiently without a knowledge of meanings. Each student must make use of meanings to express his own thoughts in his own way. 

The dictionary records many possible meanings for an entry. 

The student may choose from these in developing his own understanding of the words he meets or uses. 49

-adj. Without faults or mistakes; right; true; as, a correct answer. correct'ly, adv. cor·rect'ness, n. count'er (koun'tēr), n. 1. A person or thing that counts. 2. A piece of wood, metal, ivory, etc., used in keeping and counting a score in a game. 3. A narrow table or board in a store, restaurant, etc., on which goods for sale are handled and over which money is often passed. coun'ter (koun'ter), adv. In another or a contrary direction; in opposition; against; as, to go counter to advice. adj. Moving or acting in an opposite way; contrary; opposing. -N. The act of going counter; also, something that goes counter. -V. 1. To go, act, move, speak, etc., counter to; to oppose. 

2. To give a blow in return for one received; as, the boxer countered with his left. 

A prefix coun'ter- (koun'tĕr-). which means: 1. In the opposite direction; against; as in: coun'termarch', to march back or in the reverse direction; coun'ter·cur'rent, a current flowing against the main current. 2. In return; in retaliation; as in coun'ter-attack', to attack in return. 3. So as to undo; as in coun'ter act', to act so as to prevent something from acting in its own way; as, a drug that counteracts the effect of a poison.

53

NEW FACTS ABOUT AN OLD FRIEND 

Most students today know why they are in school. They expect to learn facts and habits there that they can use for the rest of their lives.

The dictionary habit is among the most valuable ones you will learn in school. You have been using the dictionary yourself for several years now. You find it easy to use in checking spellings, pronunciations, and meanings. 

Probably, however, you have not yet learned a number of facts that can make you a still more capable master of dictionary service. The dictionary excerpt on page 55 has been borrowed for you from A Dictionary for Boys and Girls, to help you master some new facts about dictionary service. 

1. What to Call Things in the Dictionary 

Each dictionary help has its own name. Do you know and use the ones below?

ENTRY. Each word that is explained in the dictionary is called an entry. It is entered in the proper alphabetical order, among the other entries. The word com·partment is the first entry in the dictionary excerpt. The term entry is used to mean either the word itself or the word with all the material that is given to explain it. 

RESPELLING. The second spelling of an entry, in which diacritical marks are used, is the respelling. It is placed in parentheses after the entry and is useful in pronouncing the entry correctly. The respelling for the entry com part'ment is kom pärt'měnt. 

KEY WORDS. You may check any marked letter in the respelling by finding the same marked letter in one of the words across the foot of the dictionary page. (See page 52.) These are key words, and each key word contains only one marked letter. The three key words necessary to help with the pronunciation of com·part'ment (kom. pärt'měnt) are connect, ärm, and silent. 54

PART OF SPEECH. The abbreviation in italics, fol lowing the entry and the respelling, tells what part of speech the entry is. Knowing the parts of speech, you remember, is useful when you want to use language correctly. com.part'ment (kom.pärt'- měnt), n. One of the parts into which an enclosed space is divided; a separate division or section of anything; as, the compartments of a cabinet, railway train, etc. com'pass (kum'pas); n.; pl.

COMPASSES (-ěz). 1. A device 

Compass, 1. for determining directions on the earth's surface by means of a magnetic needle which points toward the north. 2. [usually used in the plural] An instrument for drawing circles, marking measurements, etc., consisting of two pointed branches or legs joined at the top by a pivot. 3. 

Boundary; circumference; circuit; as, within the compass of the city's wall. 4.

Extent; limit; bounds; range, as of tones. 

V. 1. To make the circuit of; to enclose; to surround; as, to compass the camp with a wall. 2. To reach; to obtain; to accomplish; as, to compass a task. com pas'sion (kom păsh'un), n. Sor- Comrow or pity for another; sympathy; mercy. pass, 2. com.pas'sion.ate (kom păsh'un.it), adj. 

Disposed to pity; sympathetic. com.pas'sion ate ly, adv. 

com.pat'i.ble (kom-păt'i-b'l), adj. Harmonious; agreeable; suitable. ity (kom-păt/i-bil'i-ti), n. (kom păt'i-bli), adv. 

com.pat'i bil'com pat'i-bly com.pa'tri-ot (kom-pā'tri-ut; kom-păt'ri-ut), n. A fellow countryman.

by G. & C. Merriam Co. 

DEFINITIONS. The meanings following each entry are the definitions that help to explain it. Some entries, such as com'pass in the excerpt above, have numerous definitions. Some entries, such as com pa'tri-ot, have only a single definition. 55

ILLUSTRATIONS. The pictures found on many dic tionary pages help to illustrate one or several of the forms that an entry may have. These illustrations are especially helpful when there is a great variety among definitions, or when the entry is an uncommon or little known one. 

2. Where to Find Things in the Dictionary 

A dictionary can give you much more help than simply the spelling, pronunciation, and meanings of words. Have you ever needed to use the helps below?

ABBREVIATIONS. A dictionary always explains for you the abbreviations which are used in it. In some dictionaries this explanation is given at the foot of alternate pages; in others a list of abbreviations is given in the front of the book.

LANGUAGE RULES. A discussion of the rules of spelling, pronunciation, grammar, and the like is given at the front of most dictionaries.

SYMBOLS USED IN RESPELLING. A complete list of the diacritical marks and the letters that help with pronunciation is given in every dictionary, usually in the front.

FACTS ABOUT THE WORLD. Every dictionary contains a section devoted to information about our own country and other countries. You may find here such facts as the names of the states, the names of foreign countries, the flags of the world, the population of the world's large cities, and so on. This section is usually at the back of a dictionary. A large dictionary, of course, contains more kinds of interesting information than a smaller one can.

18. A REAL-LIFE MAGICIAN A. WRITING WORDS 

1. This is your Study List. Say these new words with your teacher. You will find all the Study List words, with their pronunciation, given in alphabetical order on pages 113 to 116. Then find the Study List words in the story. neglected magic fiction bandage discourage consult critical confidence criticize confident presence genius medical received medal mysterious mystery reception physical remedy 

All the magicians aren't dead! In fairy tale and fiction there are many, working mysterious spells with wands and magic words. But each of us knows at least one real-life magician. We hurry to consult him about our aches, pains, and bumps. He bandages our cuts and prescribes remedies for our fevers. When we are critically ill, and discouraged, just his presence brings back our lost confidence. He neglects nothing that his medical knowledge can do for us. He may never receive the medals he deserves, though it may be his genius that saves our lives. Do you know this real-life magician? He is your own physician.

19. MORE REAL-LIFE MAGIC A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. surface broadcast probable excursion poverty tower ladder strain cloud published apartment elevator publish castle elevated publisher thrill aviation suddenly aviator No fairy tale ever published is so full of magic as our own life. The poor kitchen boy in the king's castle could never have imagined apartment houses towering into the clouds. He climbed trees, stairs, and ladders on his own two feet. But it would have strained his faith to hear about an elevator that could whisk him up to the twentieth floor. Not long ago, travelers would as soon have believed in an excursion to the moon as that wingless man could leave the earth's surface and fly. Yet today aviation's first thrill is long since over. And early man would probably have fainted with fear if one of our daily radio broadcasts had been suddenly turned on. It all seems like magic!

20. CITIZENS IN THE MAKING A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. honestly fortunate legal belief annual believed feature normal handsome athletic include athletics including desirable secure gymnasium securing secured notion nonsense 

American children are very fortunate. National and local governments all include agencies for child welfare. Our citizens believe that a wise nation gives its children normal lives in security and peace. Huge sums annually provide desirable learning and living conditions for children. Playground and gymnasium athletics help young people to grow strong and handsome. Public health service is a feature in many places. Special legal machinery helps boys and girls to live honestly. The old-fashioned notion that children should be seen and not heard is considered nonsense today. Modern education helps children to be good citizens.

21. SERVING SOCIETY A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. perform performance poet chop science cocoa channel lawyer career handled groceries handling fragrant amusement amuse pilot celery amusing prose owing 

What shall you do when you grow up? The present is a good time to plan a future career. Many channels of activity are open. The world needs men and women to handle many kinds of work. We need grocers, bankers, doctors and nurses, lawyers, airplane pilots, teachers. Art and science are open to us, too. The world needs to be amused and informed. 

We need poetry, prose, and music as well as exact knowledge about the universe and its contents. The man who can raise superior celery or fragrant flowers, and the woman who can cook chops and make cocoa perfectly, may be as good citizens as the one who can perform a delicate brain operation. We owe our comfort and happiness to all kinds of workers.

22. FOODS PEOPLE LIKE A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. biscuit luncheon swamp turtle majority brook roast pigeon trout appeal timid favorably venture doubtful favorable furthermore doubtless favored odor loaf Everyone has his own favorite foods. Some are of doubtful appeal to timid appetites. The majority of people seem to enjoy such good things as brook trout and roast chicken. Not everyone looks favorably on turtle soup and pigeon pie; furthermore, few will venture to try rattlesnake meat. In the country there is nothing better than biscuits made with swamp blueberries; and the very odor of a fresh hot loaf of bread will give you an eager appetite for your luncheon. What's your favorite food?

23. BURIED TREASURE A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. abroad pearl peculiar deposit removed jewelry disturb oyster purse determine determined coin tunnel delicate issue walnut dense scarcely stumble envy Nature deposits many of her treasures in most peculiar places, as if she were determined that man should not disturb them. We stumble upon them often by accident. Priceless pearls grow in oysters, deep in the ocean. The silver for the coins in your purse, and the gold that is made into delicate ornaments, must be removed with effort from their hiding places in the earth. Costly jewels to excite envy and admiration issue from the dark ground in such rough form that one would scarcely guess their later beauty. Men tunnel in the earth for the coal we burn. They penetrate dense forests here and abroad for ebony, mahogany, walnut, and other woods for our furniture. 

Nature makes us earn her gifts.

24. OUR TROUBLESOME MONEY A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. commerce assistance terribly presume assist shelves remained pledge recall remaining introduce sorrow introduction unpaid virtue remit slightly vice resort arouse Money has caused the world both joy and sorrow, and this is strange when we recall that it was first introduced to simplify commerce. Buying and selling was once only an exchange of goods. People paid for one thing with another. We may presume that this was easy only when the carrying distance was slight. 

When a man had to send a remittance far from his loaded shelves and barns, he soon resorted to the assistance of valuable metals. These had the virtue of less weight. Later, paper slips came into use as pledges of value. A man could carry many of these while the wealth they represented remained at home. 

When the desire for money became too strong, greed and vice were aroused, and terrible things were often done. In such cases money became a curse instead of a blessing.

25. PUBLIC PROTECTION A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. review movement occasion retire commit behavior committed murder explained objection explanation innocent explaining debate angle pardon jury haste hastily sincere 

A murder trial is not a pleasant thing. And yet it is necessary. Certain individuals among our population commit unpardonable offenses against the public. One reason for our laws, lawyers, courts, and judges is to deal with such objectionable behavior. Justice in our country does not move hastily. The crime is carefully reviewed by the lawyers, and every angle is explained to the jury. Sometimes the jury members engage in a long debate after they retire to prepare a verdict. 

Though an occasional mistake is made, the verdict represents a sincere effort to determine the innocence or guilt of the accused one.

26. 

PERSONAL PROTECTION A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. moisture instrument aware acquainted preserve dock acquaintance slipper shallow sensible apology wholesale personal personally wrapped muddy sneeze scissors outlined particular The problem of personal safety is different from that of public protection. Most of us are aware of the need for individual safety, and even school children are acquainted with some ways to preserve it. Let us outline some. 

Carry scissors pointed down. Sharp instruments should be kept in a particular place. Be well wrapped up to play in the snow. Change moist or muddy shoes and slippers at once. One careless person can do a wholesale business in colds with just one good sneeze; don't be that person. Don't jump off the dock if you can't swim. Be sure water is shallow before you let small children play in it. An apology later won't make up for good sense now.

27. FASHIONS AND HISTORY A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. argument topic diameter argue sleeve response tendency military parallel talent impulse trousers salesman twist becoming frankly sermon frank mood overlooked Fashions are often dictated by events that make history. Those who have a talent for designing clothes attempt to parallel the moods and events of the times. 

During wars, clothes reflect a military spirit. In prosperous times, clothes have a tendency to respond to the impulse for expensive possessions. Fashions are not always becoming to the wearer. Skirts, sleeves, trousers, coats are often turned and twisted into strange and novel shapes. The diameter of the hat is a topic of great interest to salesman and customer alike. Even the church has at times frankly entered the fashion argument, and sermons have been preached against certain styles that could not be overlooked. Fashions make history and history makes fashions.

29. A FRIENDLY NOTE A. WRITING WORDS  

Then find them in the story. chairman delivered deliver dispatch decided statue decision oversight midst terms lecture lawn copies museum cordially confess mayor mailing gladly passage 

Dear Mary, Through some oversight last week, Mother neglected to give you those copies of "Men and Mummies" for your mother. So we're dispatching them by mail. I hope they'll be delivered in time for the lecture at the museum.

The whole city is in the midst of discussing the new statue that the mayor will give the library at the end of his term of office. We can't decide between one for the lawn and one for the wide entrance passage. Dad is the committee chairman, and I confess we'll be glad when it's over. I'll let you know what we get. 

Yours cordially, Susan

30. A NEWS LETTER A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. shortly skeleton photograph puncture homesick decline resign breeze burst shove hoarse bundle blanket roar straighten returned eve strap returning temper 

Dear Bill,

The Scouts have returned from their overnight hike. It went all right, with nobody homesick or hurt. On the eve of starting we were resigned to staying at home. The day's breeze grew to a gale with a hoarse roar, and we thought of forest fires. During the night it declined, though.

We started shortly before lunch, made the big pine by two, and stopped to straighten blanket rolls and straps. The trip's first upset occurred when one bundle burst open and Wicky lost his temper picking things up. Another accident happened when Bud punctured the football by shoving the tip of the lacer into it. Here's a photograph of the bunch. 

Skinny Briggs looks like a skeleton, doesn't he? 

Hope you can go next time. Jack

31. THE TRACK MEET A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. samples you'd advised palm dodge platform announced announcement announce sack somehow orphan tournament wwww spoil await awaiting regards regarding tennis vision

We're impatiently awaiting next Saturday. The seventh grade is invited to a track meet at the orphans' home. Bill Smith, who lives there, made the announcement regarding it from the platform this morning. There will be a tennis tournament, sack races, potato races, dodge ball and baseball, and swimming. In fact, you'd wonder how we'll have time for so many sports, but we'll get them in somehow. 

From the sample games we've played with the home, we fear they may walk off with the palm. But we advised Bill to warn them not to count on spoiling our record. We have a vision of victory ourselves!

32. THE POWER OF WORDS A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. acid badge statement comment stated scream stating obedience bond obedient maiden tremble forgive directed director journal stationery horrible dreadful ornament 

Words are useful but often dangerous. Men have long known the horror and dread that can follow a quiet comment. A simple statement can bring instant obedience, make a guilty man tremble. A harmless bit of stationery with one or two words on it has been known to cause screams of pain as if it were burning acid. On the other hand, a word like forgive can turn misery into happiness.

Language is equally the badge of the bondsman and maidservant and the ornament of the great man. 

It is an open journal in which we may read the truth about all men. What gives words this terrible power over human beings? Man's power to think directs the meaning he finds in words. It is his mind that gives words their power for good and evil.

33. WORD ANCESTORS A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. janitor reliable umpire description delayed display described trace Christian tribe tractor imitate engage energy senate engagement endure senator engaged insist When early man first began to talk, his words were the names of things around him. Next he added words to describe his acts or to imitate sounds. There was a long delay before feeling words were added. 

The English language borrowed many words from the early tribes. Some of our words still display traces of their ancestry. 

Trace and tractor both grew from a word meaning draw. Christ came from the Greek word for anointed. 

A janitor meant a doorkeeper, and senate came from a word meaning old man. Our words engage, endure, rely, energy came from words meaning pledge, harden, bind together, work. An umpire once was the odd man in counting out, and to insist meant to stand in or on. A big dictionary gives many word ancestors.

34. LETTERS AND SOUNDS A. WRITING WORDS 

Then find them in the story. carbon commencement combination contrast cushion cashier charged convince convention closing cunning consist closet caution consent canoe cabinet contact camera congratulate

Spoken words consist of sound combinations. We understand speech because of its contrasted or cunningly blended sounds. But writing words soon convinces us that we must know letters as well as sounds, for there are many unconventional things about English spelling. It's wise to be cautious, and we may congratulate ourselves if we own a good dictionary to help us. Here are pairs of words that might be troublesome: cushion, Russian; close, froze; commence, dense; closet, deposit; canoe, toe; cashier, sheer; contact, attacked; consent, meant; charged, character; carbon, care; cabinet, cable; camera, came.

34. GROWING WITH THE DICTIONARY 

You will find that your dictionary is a life tool. In fact, the more you learn, the more you will need dictionary help in explaining it. As your ideas and information grow, you will find the dictionary service growing, too. 

1. Spelling and Pronunciation 

The dictionary is the authority for both correct writing and correct speaking. Each entry is spelled as it should be written, and respelled as it should be pronounced. The syllables and the accent marks are shown in both spellings. 

In the respelling, diacritical marks indicate the sounds the letters should have. The key words at the foot of the dictionary page indicate the sounds of the marked letters. 

2. Derived Word Forms 

A very common way to make a word do extra work is to change it by adding a prefix or a suffix, or both. These derived word forms are often easy to learn, and students learn many of them without being taught. The dictionary gives help in making sure of the correct spelling and pronunciation of such derived word forms. 

3. Meanings 

Every person uses language in his own way. Most words have many different meanings, which depend on how the words are used and how they are put together in sentences. 

The dictionary gives several kinds of valuable help here. 

Many possible definitions are listed after each entry, and the student chooses the one that will be most useful in expressing or understanding thoughts. Illustrative examples are given for many entries, to show how the entry might be used in a sentence. 

Synonyms or antonyms, or both, are often given, to provide additional words that express nearly the same or nearly opposite ideas. The part of speech is given after each entry, to show what kind of work it can do in speech or writing. 

4. Derivations from Other Languages

English is a combination of words borrowed from many other languages, both ancient and modern. The dictionary gives very briefly the history of such borrowed words. It shows which language or languages lent them to English, how the borrowed words grew into other words, and what their early meanings were. 107

Syn. Cunning, crafty, artful, sly, wily agree in implying an aptitude for attaining an end by secret or devious means. Cunning implies skill, esp. in getting around a person or difficulty. 

Crafty implies more secret devices than cunning, and often suggests a skill at deception acquired by experience. 

Artful implies more indirectness of dealing; as, artful insinuations. Sly implies a somewhat vulgar turn for what is covert or double; as, words of sly import. Wily implies the subtle use of stratagems, esp. in attack or defense; as, the wily fox. -Ant.

ne'er ... never o'clock.. ..of the clock . MAKING OUR WORK EASIER 

While we have been learning to spell, we have been learning also a few rules that can make our work easier. It will be wise to remember them and to keep them in a special place where we can easily refer to them. Here they are. 

1. PLURALS 

a. Plurals of most nouns are made by adding s to the singular forms. boy, boys bill, bills night, nights b. To make pronunciation easier, the plurals of some nouns are made by adding es instead of s. dress, dresses dish, dishes c. A noun ending in y after a consonant forms its plural by changing the y to i and adding es. baby, babies city, cities lady, ladies d. A few nouns make their plurals by changing their form, usually by changing the vowel. foot, feet mouse, mice 

2. CONTRACTIONS 

man, men calf, calves

An apostrophe is used to show the omission of a letter or letters in a contraction. can not, can't you will, you'll you are, you're 

3. ABBREVIATIONS 

An abbreviation is always followed by a period. 

Mister, Mr. 

Company, Co. pound, lb. 

4. POSSESSIVES 

Friday, Fri. yard, yd. gallon, gal. a. The possessive of a singular noun is formed by adding the apostrophe and s. 

John, John's mother, mother's dentist, dentist's b. The possessive of a plural noun ending in s is formed by adding only the apostrophe. boys, boys' girls, girls' 119

MAKING OUR WORK EASIER 5. SILENT E 

a. A word ending in silent e usually drops the e before a suffix beginning with a vowel. freeze, freezes cure, curable lose, losing chose, chosen b. A word ending in silent e usually keeps the e before a suffix beginning with a consonant. sure, surely advance, advancement nine, ninety care, careful, careless 

6. WORDS ENDING IN Y 

a. A word ending in y after a vowel usually keeps the y before a suffix. day, days obey, obeyed buy, buying b. A word ending in y after a consonant usually changes the y to i before a suffix, except a suffix beginning with i. try, tried (trying) fly, flier (flying) marry, marries (marrying) 

7. DOUBLING THE FINAL CONSONANT 

a. A one-syllable word ending in one consonant (except x) after one vowel usually doubles the consonant before a suffix beginning with a vowel. big, bigger, biggest run, runner, running b. A word of more than one syllable ending in one consonant after one vowel usually doubles the consonant before a suffix beginning with a vowel, if the accent is on the last syllable. be gin', beginner, beginning ad-mit', admitted, admittance

GUIDE TO SPELLING PROGRESS A GLOSSARY FOR 

"GRADE SEVEN" This glossary was made to help you learn to use your dictionary. It has some of the things you will find in a real dictionary.

The spelling words of all the Study Lists in this book are given in dictionary order. The entry for each of these has at the end a number in brackets like this [23], to tell you the unit in which it is to be studied. Some other words are also given, to help you with your spelling words. There are no numbers in brackets after entries for these other words. 

For all entries, the spellings and respellings were taken from 

Webster's Students Dictionary for Upper School Levels, copy right 1938, by G. & C. Merriam Company, Springfield,

Massachusetts. For most words, illustration of their use is shown in sentence context. In the case of homonyms, homographs, words often confused, and words with more than one accepted spelling, the complete dictionary entries are taken, by permission, from Students Dictionary. You will want to own a real dictionary yourself as soon as you can. 

TO HELP YOU WITH PRONUNCIATION 

The first spelling in heavy black type shows you how to write the word. The respelling or respellings in parentheses will help you to say the word correctly.

In the respellings, marks have been used with some of the letters to show what sounds these letters have. The key words across the foot of each left-hand page will give you help in using these marked letters to pronounce words. 

Here is a list of marked letters used with your spelling words and the sounds you should give them: 121

ā as in save a as in always or in the first syllable of vacation â as in câre ǎ as in băg ǎ as in another a as in farm ȧ as in basket à as in ågo ē as in bē ē as in here è as in become ĕ as in fěd ĕ as in often ĕ as in after i as in find

as in is ĭ as in family ō as in hōme ō as in polite as in north ŏ as in hop as in dog 

Ŏ as in forget 

00 as in too oo as in book ou as in out oi as in noise ū as in ūse û as in figure û as in tûrn ŭ as in fun ŭ as in circus ü as in menü sh as in shall ch as in chair g as in go ng as in thing th as in that th as in thank tù as in picture dú as in education y as in yes zh = z as in azure 

TO HELP YOU UNDERSTAND MEANINGS 

Most words have more than one meaning. To help you with meanings in this glossary, most of the words are defined and some are used in sentences.

Words do different kinds of work in sentences. Abbreviations, such as adj., n., and v., are used in those entries taken from the dictionary to show how the word may be used. 

At the foot of each right-hand page after page 123, these abbreviations are explained. Your teacher will tell you about these abbreviations when you are ready to use them. 122

Goods; possessions; as to gather up one's effects. 3. v. t. 1. To bring about; to accomplish; as, he effected his pur- 

Syn. Effect, execute, perform, discharge, pose. 2. To produce; make. accomplish, achieve agree in the idea of carrying out to a proposed end. To effect is to bring about, often in the face of resistance; as, the passage was effected by force. To execute is to carry into effect, often formally; as, to execute a decree. To perform often implies protracted or regular activity; as, to perform our tasks well. Discharge implies an obligation; as, to discharge a duty. To accomplish is to complete, often by perseverance or skill. 

Achieve suggests an undertaking of difficulty or importance. dock (dok). 1. The ship was put in a dock for repairs. 2. Our ship was brought in to the dock by tugs.

1. In a fair manner; pleasantly, courteously, justly, promisingly, clearly, etc. 2. Evenly; squarely; as, he was struck fair between the eyes. 

Archaic. A fair woman; a sweetheart; also, the female sex. -n. 

Syn. Just, honest, unprejudiced. — Fair, impartial, unbiased, disinterested imply freedom from undue influence. Fair implies, negatively, absence of injustice or fraud, positively, the putting of all things on a just footing. Impartial implies absence of favor for one party more than the other. Unbiased expresses even more strongly lack of prejudice.

Disinterested denotes that freedom from bias due to absence of selfish interest. fair (fâr), n. [OF. feire (F. foire), fr. L. feria holiday, pl. feriae days of rest, holidays, festivals.] 1. I am preparing. [1] pres'ence (prěz'ĕns; -'ns), n. [OF., fr. 

L. praesentia; see PRESENT.] 1. Act, fact, or condition of being present; opposed to absence. 2. The immediate vicinity of a person; nearness.

The person or personality of an individual, esp. of a sovereign. 4. A person's bearing, carriage, or appearance; mien; as, a man of fine presence. 

An apparition. 3. 5. [18] present a play. 3. To make a gift to; as, they presented him with a book. 

4. To put before a person for acceptance; to offer as a gift; as, they presented a book to him: to bestow, esp. formally. 5. To exhibit or offer to view or notice; as, to present a fine appearance; to submit for consideration or action; as, to present a plan. 6. To aim, point, or direct, as a weapon. pres'ent (prěz'ěnt; -'nt), n. A thing presented; a gift. 

Syn. Gift, present, donation, gratuity, boon.

Gift applies to anything given; a present is always intended as a compliment or expression of good will. Donation now suggests esp. a gift to some public institution; a gratuity is a gift (usually of money) to an inferior, commonly for some service. Boon (properly denoting a gift asked) is now esp. applied to any benefit or advantage regarded as freely bestowed. pre-sent'ed (pre-zěn'těd). 1. Barclay presented his visitors to the Scout leader. 2. The finished decorations presented a satisfying brilliance.

Presence sounds almost the same as presents, but its spelling and meanings are different. 

There were presents for everybody on the Christmas tree. pres'ent (prěz'ěnt; -'nt), adj. [OF., fr. 

L. praesens, -entis, that is before one, pres. part. of praeesse to be before, fr. prae- + esse to be.] 1. Being before, in view, or at hand; being in a certain place and not elsewhere; opposed to absent. 2. Now existing, or in process; not past or future; as, the present king. 3. Obs. Immediate; instant. 4. Grammar. Expressive of action or being in the time that now is or the time of speaking, in present tense (I see you), also of habitual action or general truth (the sun shines every day), in present infinitive (to be, to do), and in present participle (being, doing). The present tense used in telling of past events is the historical present. - n. 1. Present time. 2. Present occasion or affair.

Any flat, extended surface attached to an axis and moved by the wind; as, the vanes of a windmill; hence, a fixture revolving in a manner resembling this, and moving in or by water, air, or any fluid; as, the vanes of a propeller. 3. The web or flat expanded part of a feather.

2. 4. wrap (răp), v. t.; WRAPPED (răpt); 

WRAPPING. [ME. wrappen.] 1. To cover by winding or folding; to infold. 2. To enclose; esp., to enclose in paper, a bag, etc., and tie up; as, to wrap groceries; also, to involve; entangle, engross; as, he was wrapped up in his plan. 3. To conceal by enveloping or infolding; hide. 4. To fold; as, to wrap one's napkin. v. i. To coil or twine so as to encircle or cover something. - n. 1. A blanket. 2.

A garment, as a shawl, piece of fur, etc., intended to be wrapped around a person. pictures in old cloth. wrapped (răpt). 1. We wrapped the
